#7273. Playfly’s CEO explains why leagues are looking to consolidate local sports rights

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The gradual erosion of the power of local sports through the regional sports network system — brought on largely by cord-cutting that’s seen cable and MVPD penetration drop to well below 40% of all TV homes — has led many sports fans to struggle to find their favorite local baseball, hockey or basketball team.

That confusion, one would think, might lead to depressed ratings and a struggle to secure ad revenue for those respective teams. Not so, insists Craig Sloan, CEO of Playfly Sports, a sports rep firm that deals with 83 teams across those three professional sports, and is expanding into selling OOH, experiential and other media around teams and leagues.

Sloan noted the continued strong ad marketplace for sports, which formed the single strongest line of content to be sold in this year’s upfront marketplace. He said he also expects streaming to continue to increase its share of sports viewership as linear TV sees its share erode further.

The bankruptcy of Main Street Sports Group (MSSG) earlier this year forced 20 NBA and NHL teams to change their TV and streaming partners, and eight more changed their streaming home. Throughout, Playfly kept 100% of its rights and is selling local media for all 83 of its teams this season, landing local ad sales deals with several media channels, including TV station groups Sinclair, Scripps, Gray and Nexstar, along with sports streamer DAZN.

Sloan spoke with Digiday about the health of the marketplace and the impact of streaming on viewership and ad sales.

The following Q&A has been lightly edited for space and clarity.

It’s been a slow progression of how people have started engaging. The amount of people that are now watching through streaming for our local games across the country is not insignificant. It is in the mid to high teen [percentages] of total audience for any particular game, and we see a path in the not too distant future where over a third of the audience will come in through streaming. The remainder will be on traditional linear TV. That is all predicated on the idea that traditional linear TV looks the same in three to five years from now, which I would not be betting on.

The leagues [NHL, NBA and MLB] are more interested than ever before about consolidating their local rights into a centralized platform. Whether that happens or not — whether the teams will want that or whether fans will have a say in that or not — remains to be seen. It’s all being discussed at the present.

We have been in discussions with each one of the leagues individually about that potential and what they’re thinking that might be best to happen there. So no firm decisions. The NBA probably is the first decision because they’ve been talking about doing this in a consolidated platform potentially as soon as the 27-28 season, meaning next season.

The NBA is thinking about creating a platform with potentially one of the streaming partners to consolidate all 30 teams into one platform for their local games. So you would still only get your local game, but you’d go into one environment and you’d pick up your Knicks or whoever. The league hasn’t thought that they would have every team in the product, but they’re still figuring that out.

If they can get the majority of the teams to opt in, they can go to market with something that a that a very much a streamer would likely bid on, and then they can add other teams in if teams want to eventually opt in.

The teams might push back and say we want to keep it the way that it is, and they might win. The league might say we’re going to give up on the opportunity. But I think they’re clearly going down a path of exploring this potential option. Because everybody’s end goal is to create an environment where the fans know where the games are, it’s widely distributed so everyone has access to it, and engagement levels remain high, so that the the sport continues to grow.

We’ve kept the marketplace — advertisers and agencies — focused in on the importance of this space, and to not get caught up in the soap opera and the drama of where are they going to go? At the end of the day, the games will always be on. We’ve been able to still transact in the upfront process, [as well as] scatter, doing sponsorship level deals, [and] highly integrated partnerships — and nothing’s been disrupted thankfully. So we have not had a degradation of our narrative and/or our results.

We are sitting here on a weighted average because we have every team, so some winners, some losers. Every year, like an annuity, we get to bring out a similar, if not improved, supply. One of the calling cards for us in the marketplace is although the media rights holder might change, the fans are going to show up no matter what.

In general it’s been an okay marketplace, heavily predicated in the sports world — and probably overall —on football spending: NFL first, college football second, and then everything else in sports has fallen behind that.

The scatter marketplace never materialized … In today’s environment, you have to still count quite a bit on a healthy upfront to do well in a in a calendar year. From that perspective, we would still like to see more of a return to scatter. There were certain brands this year that decided to sit out the upfront and said that they just like the optionality of coming in in scatter, and that’s just starting to materialize.

AI certainly is, and we’ve got now this prediction marketplace. Sports betting is sort of settling in, but dependent upon what the leagues will continue to allow in their rulings for for what we can sell, that could be a very healthy category for us between Polymarket, Kalshi and others. Tourism and travel are back on the upswing as are financial services which are up significantly.

The e-commerce giant is central for many consumer brand advertisers, but measurement changes could throw a wrench into their plans for next year.

As AI automates advertising workflows, the contest is shifting to who can make the decisions that deliver results.

#7271. D+ Research: Marketers express revenue optimism for 2026 holiday season despite economic pressures

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This research is based on unique data collected from our proprietary audience of publisher, agency, brand and tech insiders. It’s available to Digiday+ members. More from the series →

This is an excerpt from our Digiday+ Research report The 2026 guide to holiday marketing strategies, including A-Frame Brands, Mastercard and Ritual, which examined which commerce channels are dominating marketers’ holiday strategies. The report is based on a survey of 90 brand and retailer professionals, as well as individual interviews with marketing executives.

In Digiday+ Research’s holiday report last year, brands and retailers expressed conservative expectations for their 2025 holiday revenue. This year, however, marketers are more optimistic. The majority of survey respondents (76%) said they expect their 2026 holiday revenue to increase 1% to 31% or more in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue.

Digiday also asked respondents if they expected their 2026 holiday revenue to decrease in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue. No respondents (0%) said they expected their holiday revenue this year to decrease by more than 11% in comparison to last year.

While survey respondents were optimistic about their holiday revenue expectations, some of the marketing executives Digiday spoke with said inflation is weighing on their minds.

“It is getting brutal out there,” said Ari Bloom, founder and CEO of brand incubator A-Frame Brands. “Costs are getting so high, and the economy is soft right now, and probably will get softer as we get into the fall. The cost issue is going to be really hard for a lot of smaller companies to absorb with heavy discounting.”

Bloom added that the political calendar may play a role in holiday spending as well. “When you’re in an election year, you’ve got a different dynamic because there’s a lot of discussion around inflation,” Bloom said. “People will have a sentiment of whether the environment is inflationary, whether things are too expensive or there’s an affordability crisis.”

Laura Brodie, chief growth officer at supplement company Ritual, said she too is concerned about consumer confidence but remains optimistic overall. “Even with the state of the economy and consumer sentiment sitting at a pretty low point in history, I’m bullish,” Brodie said. “Spending is resilient. It’s less about what happens in holiday spending growth year over year, which will probably be modest. Where the spend actually gets allocated will be interesting to see.”

Brodie said she expects consumers to spend more on experiences this holiday season. “We’re seeing a big cultural shift towards nostalgia, being intentional and spending time with family,” she said. “I anticipate people will gravitate towards gifts as experiences or things that feel personal rather than mass purchased. That includes brands that have a distinct point of view, or are mission driven or founder-led. Especially in health and wellness, brands that really speak to a consumer will have a strong opportunity to win.”

While survey respondents expressed optimistic revenue expectations for the 2026 holiday season, the majority of brands and retailers said they don’t have plans to offer deeper holiday discounts this year compared to last year. Eighty-one percent of brand and retailer pros said that they expect their 2026 holiday discounts to be about the same as their 2025 discounts.

Instead of offering deeper discounts, some brands and retailers are turning to other promotional strategies like bundling products or adding a gift with purchase to entice customers. A-Frame’s Bloom said that economic pressures, including higher transportation and fuel costs, present an additional challenge for the 2026 holiday season.

“This year will be more sensitive than last year because the economic situation is more difficult and prices are higher,” he said. “We’ve been talking a lot about bundling versus discounting because holiday is a time when people will buy multiples. They might buy the same gift for three different people, and they may buy it for themselves to try on top of it.”

Bloom added that offering a gift with a purchase can encourage holiday shoppers to become repeat buyers. “We recommend products, even if it’s trial sizing, that are able to be thrown in a bag,” Bloom said. “It is a really good way to get people who are buying a gift to keep something for themselves as part of the transaction. There’s ways for brands to think about getting more value out to the customer.”

Ritual’s Brodie said offering perks to existing customers during the holiday season encourages brand loyalty and can temper the expense of new customer acquisition. “Offering exclusivity to existing customers and to pull people back with something that feels unique, a compelling gift with purchase or seasonal drop.  … The cost of acquisition is high during holiday, so being able to tap an existing audience can be cost-effective.”

She explained that Ritual has also partnered with other wellness brands to cross-promote products and provide a gift with purchase during the holidays. “Brands that are in similar sizes can come together and create something that feels compelling that can benefit both brands,” Brodie said. “We’ve partnered with like-minded brands like [skincare brand] Osea and swapped gift with purchases.”

Susan Grossman, evp of consumer acquisition and engagement services at Mastercard said in an email that she expects brands to invest in rewards, exclusive access and experiences that extend beyond the holidays this year. “The more sustainable approach is to think about value more broadly. That could be a personalized offer, exclusive access, relevant recommendations, a seamless experience, or an incentive timed to the right moment,” Grossman said.

“But to do that, you need better signals and smart personalization,” she added. “When brands understand intent, they can be more precise with when, where, and how they use incentives. Instead of blanket discounting, they can create value for the consumers who need an extra nudge while continuing to build long-term loyalty with those who already have an affinity for the brand.”

#7270. AI-derived audience insights hurt results: What the numbers say

The majority of marketing leaders have encountered inaccurate or misleading AI findings, according to GWI research.

From eliminating mundane tasks to the creation of full-fledged campaigns, artificial intelligence has become a central focus for marketers as departments feel the effects of budget constraints. Nearly 90% of senior industry professionals use AI daily, according to a survey by GWI. However, the technology isn’t without its drawbacks, with 80% of respondents saying they frequently encounter inaccurate or misleading AI findings.

“Broadly, everyone is using AI, everyone feels very confident and good about it, but everyone also has very strong concerns and suspicions, and remarkably high numbers are seeing negative impact or less than good outputs. But we're carrying on using it regardless,” said Jason Mander, chief insight officer at GWI.

“Artifical Confidence: The AI Trust Gap,” includes responses from 1,883 senior insights, agency and marketing professionals based in the U.K., U.S. and Singapore collected in the third quarter of 2026. All respondents worked at companies with over 500 employees or $26.5 million (20 million pounds) in annual turnover in areas such as media, agencies, gaming, retail and others.

While AI is known to produce incorrect information, only 36% of survey respondents say they verify AI insights “most of the time.” Not only can AI insights be incorrect, they are often out-of-date, with 72% agreeing that the technology can make something seem more relevant than it actually is. Overconfidence has consequences: 63% of respondents say they have accidentally used insights they later realized were out of date and 68% think their colleagues are doing the same thing without noticing.

“We've got somewhat lost in the buzz and the excitement, and haven't been as rigorous as we otherwise would be,” said Mander. “I think part of that is a fear of not keeping up. This adoption has been so rapid and so public and so well known that there's a pressure on professionals that if you're not using AI, you feel like you're behind the curve and you might be losing out to competitors.”

AI models can over rely on stereotypes when it comes to audience profiles and 76% industry leaders think the tech can reinforce outdated assumptions. Nearly 70% of respondents have seen the technology fail to accurately reflect their organization’s target audience in profiles. Despite this, 81% said they feel confident in using AI for audience profiles and segmentation, and 84% believe the technology can accurately explain human behavior, per the report.

The technology is also seen as largely unable to decipher consumers' motivations, with 71% of respondents saying AI can tell you “what” but not “why.” Eighty percent said that fully understanding motivations still requires human involvement and 75% believe AI will never replace direct human research. However, over 90% of organizations are building or already using AI tools that depend on external data.

While AI has been seen as a potential solution to budgetary constraints, not thoroughly checking its outputs can have real effects on business functioning. Over half of senior decision-makers have made a significant business decision based on AI insights that turned out to be incorrect or misleading, per the report.

Feeling the negative impacts of inaccurate AI insights is common, with 82% of marketing leaders feeling at least one adverse outcome, the most common being internal rework (38%), followed by delayed decision making (36%) and customer complaints (34%).

Putting too much trust in AI carries its own set of risks and 21% of survey respondents say they have seen weaker campaign performance due to mistakes made by the technology. Other observed adverse outcomes include financial loss (27%) and reputational damage (26%).

“I think we've got a little bit lost in all of the benefits because AI…allows you to become so much more productive, save so much time, increase all of your efficiencies. You perhaps haven't scrutinized some of the downsides,” said Mander. “But it also suggests that, generally speaking, companies haven't put the type of rigorous frameworks in place that you would expect with a new type of technology.”

Correction: A previous version of this article misstated the name of the report. It has been updated with the correct name, “Artificial Confidence: The AI Trust Gap.”

#7269. The future of CTV lies in standardization

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Jessica Cobbe, svp, product – supply platform, Nexxen

For years, advertisers have focused primarily on in-stream video in CTV, treating the smart TV home screen as secondary real estate. But that dynamic is quickly changing, as this year’s NewFronts made clear. As viewers spend more time navigating content recommendations and streaming apps before pressing play, the home screen is becoming one of the highest-attention environments in media. The challenge here is that the infrastructure surrounding it still feels stuck in the past, lacking both nuance and standardization.

As CTV enters its next phase of growth, however, standardization will become one of the most important priorities shaping its future.

Unlike other digital or programmatic channels, native CTV inventory (including the home screen) has historically varied widely across device manufacturers, from creative specifications and ad formats to click actions and activation workflows. In practice, this means advertisers and agencies are forced to navigate a patchwork of technical requirements to achieve scaled reach. Consequently, adoption has been relatively slow, not because marketers question the value of this inventory, but because operational complexity often outweighs the benefits.

Without standardized industry specifications, preparing one native CTV campaign for multiple OEM environments can easily require 10 to 30-plus hours of prelaunch adaptation. For a single native CTV campaign, advertisers and creative teams may need to adapt the same asset package across 10 to 15 or more unique placements before launch. When dimensions, UI requirements, copy limits, image recomposition, QA and more complex executions like animated or pause ads all vary by environment, that work can add up quickly.

Worse yet, inconsistency impacts creative quality. The more time teams spend reworking assets for each environment, the harder it becomes to maintain a unified brand experience across touchpoints. Campaigns perform best when creative is designed intentionally for a given environment while still delivering consistency across the broader media plan, and standardization helps create the technical foundation that makes this possible.

Fragmented infrastructure also creates barriers to broader programmatic activation. Many buyers and planners expect the same efficiencies from CTV native inventory that they already experience across other digital channels, like display (think: streamlined workflows and scale). When inventory requires excessive customization or manual execution, it becomes harder to integrate naturally into a broader media strategy.

Standardization is not about reducing differentiation among OEMs. Rather, it’s about creating the connective tissue that allows advertisers to participate more efficiently across the ecosystem, and that efficiency benefits everyone involved. Advertisers gain simpler activation and more scalable reach. Creative teams can spend less time adapting assets and more time focusing on storytelling. Agencies can integrate native CTV inventory more seamlessly into omnichannel planning. And OEMs can make their inventory more accessible to a broader range of buyers.

As the CTV market continues to mature, operational simplicity is becoming just as important as premium supply. And for smart TV home screens, standardization may prove to be the unlock that moves this inventory from a promising opportunity to an outcome-driving media channel.Partner insights from Nexxen

The e-commerce giant is central for many consumer brand advertisers, but measurement changes could throw a wrench into their plans for next year.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends.

#7266. Twitch debuts shoppable, skippable ads as TwitchCon feels growing pains

A pair of new ad products unveiled around the gathering are meant to shore up the livestreaming service’s ability to meet full-funnel marketing needs.

New ad launches for Twitch are part of Amazon’s efforts to turn the livestreaming service into a full-funnel advertising channel while shoring up its image as a place that consistently attracts a young, highly engaged user base. Amazon is trying to position Twitch as a destination for sustained consumer attention but TwitchCon, its signature showcase, has been wrestling with growing pains related to creators of late.

Twitch viewers spend over four hours a day on the site, on average, and many creators are known for hosting hourslong streams that feature frequent interactions with their chats. Nearly 70% of Twitch’s global audience is Gen Z and millennials, many of whom can’t be found on Prime Video, the announcement said. Ninety-two percent of ads on Twitch are seen by viewers, above averages for other social platforms, per new research that measurement firm Lumen conducted on behalf of Amazon.

Shoppable ads are one way for Amazon to demonstrate that Twitch campaigns can be tied directly to business results. The format appears as an overlay to Twitch video ads and works in a similar fashion to interactive video ads on Prime Video. Users can add products featured in the Twitch ad to their Amazon cart, browse different styles and buy directly from the e-commerce site, shortening the path from brand message to transaction. Twitch shoppable ads can be bought alongside Prime Video inventory via the Amazon Ads Agent.

Any shoppable products will already be on Amazon, meaning brands do not need to create a separate landing page or individual creative. Twitch will bring the format to Europe, the Middle East and North Africa and Asia Pacific throughout H1 next year, and later plans to introduce iterations designed for Twitch on mobile and connected TV.

Skippable ads appear to be a way for Amazon to court a wider range of brands to Twitch, since the option is promoted as being more budget-friendly. Skippable ads are self-service and performance-oriented, with advertisers only paying when a viewer remains engaged. Razer, the gaming hardware maker, saw a 2.44 times higher return on ad spend when testing the format in the U.S., about double the ROAS of prior Twitch campaigns focused on standard video. One-third of the customers reached with skippable ads campaigns were new to the brand.

TwitchCon’s status as a must-attend destination for top streamers and sponsors has been challenged due to concerns around personal safety and changing creator needs, Digiday reported earlier this week. Creator marketing has evolved from a secondary consideration for many marketers into a core media channel, and the presence of creators at other industry conferences, such as Cannes Lions and Advertising Week, has intensified.

Amazon does not break out Twitch’s revenue in earnings reports, but the e-commerce giant’s overall rate of advertising growth has slowed. Revenue derived from ad sales was up 12% year over year to $19.6 billion in Q3 2026 compared to 22% during the same period last year.

#7265. ‘We’re not trying to be TwitchTok’: Twitch adds new short-form video feature

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

For Twitch streamers, on-platform discoverability has always been a major issue. Twitch has tried to address this, with mixed success. Now, the platform is making its biggest swing yet.

Starting early next year, Twitch will let creators upload short-form videos made outside the platform. Announced during the keynote at TwitchCon 2026 in San Diego, the move is aimed at improving on-platform discovery and creating more ad inventory. Twitch’s goal isn’t to make clips into a TikTok-like destination, execs say, but to help viewers discover creators and tune into their livestreams, where creators make most of their money.

The new uploads build on Twitch’s existing Clips tools, which have let creators and viewers clip livestreams and share them on-platform for more than a decade. But clips made or shared outside the platform are now a central part of creator culture.

“It’s a smart thing for them to do. You want to follow your favorite Twitch creators but you aren’t able to catch them live at that moment, so you’re going to TikTok or you’re watching old uploads on YouTube,” said Eric Bogard, CEO of UnderCurrent Talent.

Cross-platform follower conversion is difficult, especially for streamers. “Every data point will always show you that getting people to click over different platforms doesn’t work really at all,” said Tim McArdle, co-founder and chief content officer at creative studio Wildcatter.

Twitch’s chief product officer Mike Minton said the platform had “missed an opportunity” to make clips happen on Twitch. Historically, Twitch creators’ short-form content goes viral on TikTok, X, YouTube Shorts, and Instagram, racking up millions of views.

McArdle said that, though Twitch is a bit behind the game, these features could fundamentally change the platform. “They needed to do it, and for a company that produces a lot on Twitch, we’re excited about it,” he said.

Twitch streamers Vio and Frogan were less optimistic about the new short-form push. “People don’t go to Twitch to find clips or videos. They go to YouTube or TikTok,” said Vio. “They need to let the Clips thing go. No one is going to watch clips on Twitch natively,” said Frogan. Dan “Onepeg” Marshall, who has over 72,000 followers on the platform, said it’s a good move for the overall ethos of Twitch.

Twitch already has an existing “Clips” tab on both its mobile and desktop versions, but it will also add a new “Category Recap” shelf that will appear on category pages, which will populate with clips and recaps. Marshall found the Category Recaps the most interesting feature announced, noting it could be a positive for creators in specific categories trying to catch eyeballs.

“Of all of the things Twitch has tried to pull in the last several years, this is probably one of the better ideas for discoverability if it does more than just show clips of the largest creators in each category,” he said.

With this investment in short-form content, Twitch feeds will eventually become more personalized for users, whose viewing habits will dictate whether they’re fed more short-form content or more streams, according to Minton.

“We’re not trying to be TwitchTok,” he clarified. Instead, Twitch hopes these new features will help creators get discovered on-platform, which will make it easier to funnel viewers into livestreams, which is where they make their money.

“Will people consume as much of the clips on Twitch as TikTok? Probably not, but that’s OK… the utility of why they’re doing it and how it’s going to be used is different,” said McArdle.

Twitch’s new editing tools will make it easier to find exciting moments and turn them into clips. That could be a boon for smaller creators who don’t have the money to hire an editor or the time to edit content on an external app or program after they’ve been livestreaming for hours. Vio and Marshall wondered whether AI would power these features. Vio said the existing AI tools have been “ass so far.”

Minton told Digiday he’s focused on the “torso and long tail of creators” on Twitch. A more robust creator platform with better discoverability means more creators making money.

Allowing creators to upload externally made clips, rather than just chopped up, unedited versions of their streams, adds even more inventory to the platform. “It sounds like they’re trying to capture more inventory as leverage from an advertising perspective,” said Justin Miclat, chief growth officer at talent and brand management company Click Media.

Minton said increased audiences are what advertisers care about, and that Clips can help activations organized directly between brands and creators.

For creators however, the advertiser opportunity won’t yet translate into direct revenue. Minton confirmed that, though there are ads nestled amongst clips, there’s no direct monetization for short-form on Twitch just yet.

“Twitch is mostly live monetization, so the transition from the short form into live is where you’re going to make your money,” Minton said. “70 percent of it directly from your fans versus ad-supported. I do think over time we will find ways for ads in and around clips that’ll make sense that won’t diminish the user experience but will increase those earning opportunities for creators.”

Ultimately, this short-form push should help content creators on Twitch get more eyes on their livestreams, a notoriously difficult task the platform has been struggling with for years.

“Live content isn’t amazing for discovery… You hop into [a stream], you may not understand what’s going on. It could be a dead moment in a stream, it’s just not great,” Minton said. “Whereas short form is amazing…you get a snapshot of a great moment, and you can decide: ‘Is this something I want to spend more time on?’ Yes, it is. Follow. Boom. Next time you open up the app, that streamer’s in the feed.”

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends.

Bustle Digital Group has teamed up with Profound to help brands like Pampers and Innisfree boost their visibility in AI recommendations in a new GEO-offering.

Yahoo is the first licensee of the Internet Licensing Collective, offering publishers a new way to get paid for content used in AI search.

#7263. Deloitte Digital takes the holdco operating system pitch straight to the CEO

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

CEOs have never cared much for holdcos. Enterprise solutions, on the other hand, get a meeting. Which makes Deloitte Digital’s new operating system for marketers an awkward one for the rest of the industry.

It’s sold as a license, though not the kind a CMO can approve alone. Designed eventually to stretch across sales, marketing, service and commerce, it’s the kind of deal Deloitte Digital’s U.S. CMO Mark Singer called a “more executive conversation.” That conversation happens in the C-suite, well above the marketing department where holdcos usually pitch.

The product is called Converge. It strings together AI agents, some built by Deloitte and others a client already runs elsewhere, to handle marketing and sales work from research and briefs through to campaign deployment and deal tracking. Two parts are live, a marketing operating system and a sales one, with a commerce version on the way. Singer pitched them as pieces of one front office system built around an idea the consulting firm calls “brand to cash” — every step of marketing and sales, from building a brand to closing a deal, should be traceable back to revenue.

“It’s not just about the agents, it’s about the task orchestration among them,” said Singer.

If that sounds familiar, it’s because holdcos have spent the better part of two years saying much the same. It carries more weight from a consulting firm, though, and Singer made a point of setting Deloitte’s version apart. The market is full of AI tools, he said, and “everybody’s doing a press release every 20 minutes on them.” Some exist to make an agency more efficient for its cleints. Others sell smarter work off the back of an owner’s media insights. Deloitte wants to build tools “that we hand off to our clients,” said Singer. From there, they can run them in-house or use them as a way of “rethinking their agency infrastructure,” he added.

A demo showed how that could play out. Umberto Castaldo, Deloitte Digital’s Marketing OS go-to-market lead, ran a campaign for a dummy sneaker brand. Agents passed the work along from market research and media modelling to the brief, the content, quality assurance and finally deployment into the brand’s existing mar tech. All those agents are routed into the consulting firm’s wider tech stack. Some of them draw on Deloitte’s own asset along the way. The research agent, for instance, can tap Deloitte’s own third-party data spine alongside client data.

Of those steps, content is doing a fair share of the selling, at least in the system’s current form. In the consulting firm’s view, brands have already paid for far more content than they’ve used.

Big brands have spent hundreds of millions, if not billions, on content over the past five years, Castaldo argued, and little of it has run recently. The system’s answer is to put that back catalog to work. Tagging agents can first relabel what sits in a brand’s asset library so other agents can find it. In the demo, those agents then built email and display ads for three audiences out of images and headlines the sneaker brand already had on file. Each asset is tracked from there, so the brand can see how it performs from one campaign to the next.

The sales side runs in parallel. Abe Awasthi, Deloitte Digital’s AI sales transformation leader, showed how its built around a seller’s day: the gap to quote, which deals need attention and where new demand might come from. That last job falls to an agent called Scout, which reads campaign results from the marketing side and matches them to a rep’s target customers. Administrators decide how far each agent can go, from suggesting to drafting to acting.

“Autonomy should be earned,” said Awasthi.

For now, most early clients are using the two halves separately, though Singer said some are starting to take both. Joint marketing and sales deals remain early, with one or two pitches in the past year, Singer said. As clients mature, he expects them to see the value in the “white space in between.”

The consulting firm shared one result. A client gave half of its 300 business development reps the sales operating system. Over a three-month pilot, the response rate on their outbound emails went from 17% to around 56%. Once the rollout scaled, that settled in the 30s — roughly double the starting point. Quotas for the team up 20%, said Awasthi, and the client is hiring more reps. Marketing teams are similarly seeing early gains in throughput, said Castaldo, who didn’t share any specifics.

Like everything the consulting firm has tried in advertising, Converge is designed to sit above it all, and Singer was blunt about where that leads. He expects “everything will be disrupted” across marketing services and said the shift is already underway. Four large clients he wouldn’t name are pulling their marketing into global capability centers, which he said is now possible without losing the personalization each market and language needs. Deloitte Digital already runs centralized functions like these for clients. They cover most of the marketing work, though brand advertising sits outside them. Setting up a function on that scale. Singer added, means building what he called “captive environments” for it.

In outsourcing, a “captive” is an operation a company owns and runs for itself. The brand keeps the people and the budget. The system they all work inside is another matter, and that’s what the largest marketing services groups are now chasing. Consulting firms like Deloitte have the advantage of already being in the room where that gets decided. The holdcos have been trying to get there for a while. At CES in January, Omnicom, Stagwell, Havas and WPP each unveiled agentic platforms built to sit on top of a client’s existing tools and run the work between them. WPP has since gone further, recasting itself as a single company with four divisions: WPP Media, WPP Production, WPP Enterprise Solutions and the newly-created WPP Creative. Publicis started earlier, paying $3.5 billion for Sapient in 2015 and $4.4 billion for Epsilon in 2019. Software companies are doing the same. Adobe used its conference earlier this month to introduce its end-to-end content supply chain product.

“One of the secret weapons of the management consultancy agencies in gaining market share during the past 10 years has been leveraging their established relationships with client CEOs, CIOs, and Boards of Directors,” said Gartner vp analyst Jay Wilson. “These relationships, and the credibility and sway those agencies have with those executives often allowed them to intercept opportunities before an agency review took place. Increasingly, we see and hear from clients and agencies that the CFO is the critical piece of the new business puzzle, as marketing budgets remain flat, and CMOs are pressured to prove the value of marketing, including agency investments.”

A new product manager role will explore ad-related monetization, suggesting the platform could make ads a bigger part of its business.

Marketers expect their 2026 holiday revenue to increase 1% to 31% or more in comparison to their 2025 holiday revenue.

Mattel’s TikTok Shop store debuts this week with roughly 20 products across Barbie, Hot Wheels, Fisher-Price and Monster High properties.

#7261. How Bustle Digital Group is helping Pampers and Innisfree show up in AI recommendations

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Bustle Digital Group is using Profound’s AI measurement tools to help brands, including Pampers and Innisfree, show up in AI-generated recommendations.

The publisher’s GEO offering, called ECHO internally, uses Profound analytics and AI agents trained on content from BDG’s titles to generate creative briefs and guidelines. BDG’s creative team uses those materials to produce content designed to be cited by AI platforms. At least three brands have paid for the offering.

An uptick in client inquiries this year prompted BDG to develop the offering, said Graham Howes, svp of distribution and AI intelligence at BDG, which owns titles like Bustle, Nylon, Elite Daily and Scary Mommy.

BDG is the only publisher currently working with Profound to turn this process into an advertiser offering.

In one campaign, which ran between Aug 11–Sep 30, Korean beauty brand Innisfree’s visibility across 200 tracked skincare prompts grew 30%, while Bustle’s citation rank rose from #709 to #12, according to a BDG case study based on Profound data.

“By combining trusted editorial environments, answer engine optimization, and prompt-level measurement, we were able to demonstrate that AI visibility can be measured, optimized, and ultimately transformed into a meaningful competitive advantage,” said Julien Bouzitat, gm of Innisfree U.S., part of the Amore Pacific Group.

Baby care brand Pampers moved from #2 to #1 in AI visibility for fragrance-free diaper questions following a Scary Mommy explainer addressing Swaddlers’ fragrance-free switch. The explainer ran between Sep 4–17.

“There isn’t a defined playbook for how publishers should be approaching this. So we really had to come at this from a very DIY place of first understanding the space,” Howes said.

The process begins with an RFP and conversations with the brand on their AI visibility goals, Howes explained. Then comes a two- to four-week baseline research phase, where BDG and Profound analyze eight to 10 topics that the advertiser wants to increase its visibility against within an AI answer engine, and 25 to 50 prompts [on each topic] to understand the brand’s competitive standing and what sites the answer engines are currently citing.

BDG then uses its own AI agents, trained on its titles’ content, to develop an editorial strategy for the branded content. This includes potential article topics, headline structure, and tone, Howes explained. That strategy is then presented to the advertiser.

“The advertiser has no hand in the actual creative development at all. We leave that entirely up to our editors, partly because we want to make sure that we are following all of the best practices of what gets cited, and something that is very heavy-handed on the advertorial front is very unlikely to do that,” Howes said.

AI agents within Profound then design the creative briefs, which get handed off to BDG’s creative team. They write in their own voice, but follow guidelines set by the agents, such as including an FAQ section at the bottom of the article, or the type of article format, such as a listicle or an explainer.

“You’ve got to be able to connect analytics into action,” said Josh Blyska, head of insights at Profound.

Once the content is live, it’s cross-promoted on Reddit, where BDG is running paid media. This helps build awareness among relevant communities of Reddit users, which “still matters a lot in the overall algorithm of what is being picked up and why,” Howes said. Reddit, he added, remains a significant citation source for answer engines.”

BDG tracks the performance of those posts throughout the campaign, he added.

This is the big question, especially given attribution is difficult because AI platforms offer limited insight into how people use their answers to make purchasing decisions, making it hard for publishers to attribute AI’s influence on traffic, engagement and conversions.

BDG tracks the performance of the GEO-branded content by analyzing metrics including the brand’s visibility on AI platforms, and BDG’s citation share and citation rank, mainly across ChatGPT, Gemini and Google’s AI Overviews.

Profound measures AI visibility by whether the brand appears in an AI response. Citation share measures the percentage of total citations in a given set of AI-generated answers that come from a particular publisher’s site, Blyska said. (Citation share is typically between 0.1% and 3%, he noted.) Citation rank measures how a publisher’s content ranks against competitors based on how frequently each is cited in AI-generated answers.

“We want to really be able to prove to the best of our ability, that these two goals are interconnected when we run programs in this kind of way,” Howes said.

Using Profound, BDG identifies examples of its content appearing in AI answers, monitors which articles generate the most citations and makes real-time optimizations to improve performance, Howes said. Advertisers then receive a report detailing the results.

“Now the question is, does the influence layer – i.e., ChatGPT, where people find the conviction to decide what to buy – net cleanly into the transaction layer, which is often through Google into the website? And the answer is no, not right now,” Blyska said.

Howes declined to share how much revenue its GEO offering was generating, or how much BDG was charging brands for this offering. It’s sold as a standalone offering, or bundled into existing custom content packages. Profound does not receive a cut of these deals.

“We see this more as an extension of [our] custom content opportunities, not a replacement to them,” Howes said. “Clients can still have those opportunities to reach our audiences on our sites, on our social channels, but also pair that with these types of opportunities to also reach similar audiences that are now choosing to engage an answer engine to have a question answered.”

Ashish Jacob, director of GEO at Go Fish Digital, said consistent measurement matters more than which analytics platforms a company uses to track visibility.

“The most valuable metrics include how often a brand appears in relevant AI responses, which sources are cited, sentiment, competitive share of voice, and how those results change over time. It’s also important to understand the prompts, AI platforms, and sampling methods behind the data, since different tools may produce different results. First-party reporting is becoming increasingly valuable as well,” Jacob said.

Profound is seeing some renewals for 2027, as well as exploring running always-on, yearlong campaigns, Howes said.

“A lot of advertisers realize that this content is very powerful, but it also has a half-life to it, and if you want to really make sure that you have an ongoing presence – especially when we think about our partners in beauty and fashion, where so much of what they’re doing is very seasonal – that they need to really have a more always-on approach to their content strategy,” Howes said.

These GEO offerings require publishers to be nimble, Howes said. Because AI answer engines are constantly changing their algorithms, GEO requires ongoing monitoring, research and content optimization, he said. Publishers must continually adapt their strategies to keep brands visible in AI-generated answers. But that’s an opportunity in itself, to act “not just as content partners” but as “a knowledge resource” to help brands understand what’s happening within AI answer engine recommendations, he added.

Howes sees GEO as an opportunity not just to improve its own visibility in AI search, but to grow its advertising business. Branded and custom content is BDG’s largest revenue source, he said. Measuring and demonstrating how GEO efforts benefit advertisers will be critical to strengthening brand partnerships and expanding these offerings as a revenue stream, Howes noted.

“I really want to better understand how this new paradigm we find ourselves in really plays a direct role in our business, not just our own editorial growth. To be able to have that kind of data and visibility, not just in our own efforts, but in our advertisers’ efforts as well, is fundamental to what we do because we need to be able to have that level of transparency to grow the kind of partnerships that we’re looking to grow into next year with these types of packages,” Howes said.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends.

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

Yahoo is the first licensee of the Internet Licensing Collective, offering publishers a new way to get paid for content used in AI search.

#7258. Substack job listing hints at a bigger advertising push in 2027

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Few took Substack’s co-founder and chief writing officer Hamish McKenzie literally when he told Digiday in 2025 that ads were a long way off. A new job hire suggests they should have.

The online publishing and newsletter platform is currently hiring a U.S.-based product manager of payments to help expand monetization on Substack and find better ways to support paying creators, according to the company’s careers page.

The job spec states that the scope of the role includes gifting, retention offers and improvements to the payments and subscription experience, alongside opportunities to explore commerce, and (crucially) advertising-related monetization over time. The role, which comes with a cash compensation package between $210,000 and $230,000, will also help shape future ad opportunities as the business evolves.

Substack first signalled a willingness to reconsider ads in March 2025 when Digiday spoke to McKenzie. He described ads as an “interesting business” for Substack, while suggesting it was “some way off in the distant future.”

Since making those comments, Substack has gradually started taking a more active role in connecting brands with publishers, although its efforts so far have focused on sponsorships rather than conventional ads.

Historically, ads on Substack have been creator-led rather than platform-led. Brands typically partnered directly with individual newsletters or creator networks, arranging sponsorships across newsletters and podcasts.

In June, it announced the first phase of a native sponsorship program, introducing Creator Kits to help publishers showcase their audiences and attract brand partners. In October, it introduced three sponsorship formats:

But the latest job spec suggests Substack could be considering a broader role for advertising in its monetization strategy from next year.

For some, this brings both opportunities and risks. Substack’s potential pivot toward paid advertising feels like “the end of an idealistic era,” according to Brenda Imeson, director of strategy at Brave Bison. Another platform that built its identity partly around resisting ads is now facing the commercial realities of sustaining its business.

“Subscription fatigue is biting, paywalls inevitably gatekeep good journalism and limit reach. If handled sensitively, native sponsorships will allow writers to keep more work free without hollowing out editorial integrity and could democratize access to great writing,” Imeson said. “The test will be if Substack can take brand money and keep thoughtful discourse from degrading into attention grabbing algorithmic bait.”

Substack did not respond to Digiday’s request for comment.

#7256. How brands are banking on live shopping this holiday season

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The story was first published by Digiday sibling Glossy

Many brands have accepted that, to effectively compete this holiday season, last year’s marketing playbook won’t cut it.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends. According to data from eMarketer, livestream retail sales in the U.S. are set to reach nearly $20 billion this year. And annual spend per buyer on livestreams is growing by 23% to an average of $339.27.

Brands like Tarte Cosmetics, Crocs, Beachwaver and Gap are planning to make use of live shopping, particularly on TikTok, this holiday season. Tarte and Crocs plan to launch exclusive products just for TikTok live shopping, with Crocs’ exclusives including a Disney collaboration. TikTok has seen livestream shopping sales double in the first half of this year, and use of the hashtag #holidayshopping rose 263% year over year in October.

Ziad Bon, founder of Oudware, the 3-year-old fragrance retailer that sells imported fragrances from the Middle East, told Glossy that live shopping has become an integral part of his company’s holiday strategy. Oudware already has a fairly comprehensive strategy, hosting live shopping shows on weekdays for its 215,000 followers. From Black Friday through mid-December, Oudware will be increasing its live frequency, going live on TikTok Shop every day from 2 p.m. to 7 p.m. EST. Oudware already makes more than $200,000 per month from live shopping on average and does more than $20 million in annual revenue. Its products sell for around $30-$100 per bottle.

Bon said it’s important for brands engaging in live shopping to have a plan for each live. Oudware’s live strategy is to spend the first hour of each live with what Bon called the “gift hotline,” where the team takes feedback and requests from viewers on which items and fragrances they want to see go on sale that evening. The next few hours are spent showcasing products and putting them up for sale through the regular TikTok Shop sales channel. The last hour or two of each live are reserved for auctions, a feature on TikTok Shop announced in 2025 and rolled out more broadly this year.

For Oudware, which sells about 3,000 products per day on TikTok Shop livestreams, saving the auctions for last is crucial. Live shopping conversion rates tend to be much higher than standard e-commerce, and consumers are increasingly drawn to auctions due to the possibility of lower prices. Adobe data from this year found that people are nearly 70% more likely to buy something during a live shopping stream if they see others bidding as well.

“Personally, I hate auctions,” Bon said. “A lot of our fragrance manufacturers don’t want their fragrances sold at auction, and the prices are often super low. But as a seller, it’s really hard to get engagement if you’re not doing auctions, since that’s what the customers want.”

Running auctions only at the end of the live has two purposes: It lets Oudware spend the majority of a livestream selling products at their regular prices and saves the auctions for when the live has the most viewers. More viewers means more bids, which means Oudware can sell products at auction for closer to the normal asking price.

Live shopping was a big success for brands last holiday season. TikTok Shop saw over $500 million in sales throughout the holiday period in 2025. Meanwhile, on Black Friday last year, Whatnot saw $75 million in sales in one day, tripling its previous peak from the year before.

TikTok itself is also introducing new features to support live shopping for the holidays. In the past year, big name brands and retailers like Fenty Beauty and Sephora have joined TikTok Shop. Products sold on a live will now have a visible badge if they’re part of TikTok’s Fulfilled by TikTok program (similar to Amazon Prime). Bon said the ability to ship product right after a live ends and have it arrive three days later is likely to be a big draw for customers. And, starting this fall, TikTok Shop will be working with Happy Returns to let shoppers return purchased items to any of Happy Returns’ 10,000 drop-off locations across the U.S.

“Live shopping has become one of the most engaging formats on TikTok Shop, and we expect even more brands to embrace it this holiday season as they look for new ways to connect with shoppers,” said Patrick Nommensen, president of strategic initiatives at TikTok Shop, in an email to Glossy. “What makes Live particularly compelling during the holidays is its ability to turn product discovery into an interactive shopping experience, allowing consumers to watch demonstrations, ask questions, and hear directly from brands and creators before making a purchase.”

Bustle Digital Group has teamed up with Profound to help brands like Pampers and Innisfree boost their visibility in AI recommendations in a new GEO-offering.

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

Yahoo is the first licensee of the Internet Licensing Collective, offering publishers a new way to get paid for content used in AI search.

#7255. Media Buying Briefing: Buyers grapple with incoming Amazon reporting changes

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Media Buying Briefing covers the latest in agency news and media buying for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Monday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

Changes in the way that key media platforms measure impressions mean that performance and pricing benchmarks may be thrown into disarray come the new year.

Amazon has estimated brands will see 63% fewer impressions for display ads appearing across its retail properties, including its homepage, product pages and search results, according to a notice distributed among advertisers seen by Digiday. It’s due to the platform’s decision to shift from counting impressions the moment they begin downloading to a user’s device, to when they actually render on the user’s screen, from Jan. 1. Google announced a similar transition at the beginning of September, effective Feb. 17; spokespeople from both tech companies declined to comment.

In turn, six buyers who spoke to Digiday said they’re working to amend reporting methodologies and soothe client nerves.

Buyers fear that a reduction in reported impressions could mean that supply is constrained, driving up CPMs. “If there’s going to be fewer impressions, your CPMs will be higher,” explained Hannah Schatz, vp of programmatic operations at Coegi.

At the same time, the stricter impression reporting methodology means that lower-impact display placements further down Store pages will become even less desirable for advertisers — while top-of-page slots that stand a greater chance of influencing shoppers will see an increase in demand.

“There’s going to be probably more competition on the platform,” said Nola Ladd, awareness media supervisor at media agency Collective Measures.

Amazon has not issued guidance regarding the expected impact on pricing or costs, or whether it might update its billing methodology around the new impression reporting, according to Stagwell Media Platform’s global product managing director, Dru Sil.

“The implications are up to us to discover,” he said.

While the drop in impressions might unnerve some clients, it’s unlikely to provoke advertisers to reduce spending. Sil suggested that most advertisers will maintain spending levels, in order to establish new performance benchmarks in January and February.

“I don’t expect clients to like dramatically scale up budgets to try to get ahead of the curve. You’re likely going to continue to run what you’ve been running because you have an expected outcome that you’re trying to obtain from Amazon, regardless of the specific ad format,” said Sil.

He added: “You need to build that baseline anyway, right? And the only way to build that baseline is to be running ads.”

Benchmarking may be the real problem buyers face when they shake off the new year’s hangover.

Automated reporting processes, or media mix models that use impressions as a key input, might need to be recalibrated to account for the expected drop in reported impressions. “If you have a lot of automated processes, optimizations, reconciliations, built around these data sets already, you’re going to have to do a little bit of work,” said Paul DeJarnatt, vp and head of investment at Novus.

For most buyers, Amazon’s and Google’s tinkering with impression reporting merely underlines the importance of focusing on outcomes, rather than media KPIs, to understand campaign performance.

“The bottom line is, we don’t expect this to change campaign results. The shift will make reporting metrics look different, but it shouldn’t move ROAS or CPA, which are the outcomes our clients care about,” said Jared Smith, senior director of strategy and innovation, January Digital.

“Impressions, CPMs, they shouldn’t be north star metrics,” said Ernie DeColte, svp of media at indie agency Level. “They’re tool metrics we use to optimize towards business impact. This might be a wake-up call to a few advertisers. Maybe it’s time to think about buying differently.”

Still, communicating this change to clients who might be alarmed when they see impression counts fall off a cliff (or CPMs spike) is something media buyers across the industry are attempting to get ahead of.

“The client, even though that metric is misleading, is used to seeing a big number,” said Ladd.

“They’re used to seeing really low CPMs. They’re used to seeing really great impression volume, and that change and that difference is going to require agencies and media experts to really think about the way that they present success to the client,” she added.

Just because it’s got a donut graph doesn’t mean a report is saying very much. According to an Experian survey of 400 marketers, the profusion of dashboards and data isn’t helping marketers back up their decisions.

“In the past, a company like Unilever, buying big media in Brazil from TV Globo, had a significant competitive advantage. Now you are buying from Meta or from Google or from Amazon or from Walmart or from TikTok, and that model doesn’t favor a scale of purchase. It favors the quality of engagement you generate on the platform.”

—Unilever CEO Fernando Fernandez, speaking during its Capital Markets Day last week, on why better creative could be the secret to making its marketing operation work harder.

Local sports rights have gotten more complex and confusing as the regional sports network business changed. The leagues are now looking to simplify that offering.

As AI automates advertising workflows, the contest is shifting to who can make the decisions that deliver results.

#7254. Director of Technology Partnerships at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Director of Technology Partnerships to lead the teams responsible for growing Supabase through technology integrations and our marketplace ecosystem.

You’ll bring together two complementary motions: partnerships that embed Supabase into the products developers already use, and a marketplace that helps developers discover and connect complementary technologies to their Supabase projects. Your job is to turn both into durable sources of revenue, adoption, and customer value.

Reporting to the Head of Partnerships, you’ll lead and develop the Integrations and Marketplace teams, set their commercial strategy, and work hands-on with our most important partners. A central part of the role is working closely with AI labs to build strategic relationships, develop integrations, and bring joint opportunities to market. You’ll partner closely with Product, Engineering, Sales, Marketing, and Finance to take integrations from idea to launch—and grow their impact long after launch.

This role is based in the San Francisco Bay Area, where you’ll build relationships with leading AI, developer tools, and infrastructure companies. Supabase is remote-first, and you’ll work across a globally distributed team.

Lead, coach, and develop the Integrations and Marketplace partnerships teams, including team leads and partner managers.

Set a clear strategy for where Supabase invests across strategic technology partnerships, marketplace categories, and emerging ecosystem opportunities.

Experience growing both a small number of strategic partnerships and a broader, scalable partner program.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

#7246. Beyond Points and Perks: How Sephora Makes 46 Million Members Feel Known

A new retail battleground is emerging and it’s the AI assistant deciding what to recommend before a shopper ever visits either one. That makes loyalty more valuable, not less.

Emmy Brown Berlind, SVP and general manager of loyalty at Sephora, joins Matt Britton to explore how a nearly 20-year-old loyalty program is adapting to that shift.

Their conversation covers why the Beauty Insider program is designed to feel personal instead of transactional, and how Sephora uses AI without crossing into “creepy.”

She also discusses how its combination of first-party data, beauty advisors, stores, and emerging agentic shopping partnerships could help the brand stay relevant wherever beauty discovery moves next.

Emmy Brown Berlind is SVP and general manager of loyalty at Sephora, where she leads the Beauty Insider program serving more than 46 million members. She has spent more than a decade at Sephora across innovation, content, commerce, and loyalty, giving her a broad view of how the brand connects stores, digital experiences, rewards, creators, personalization, and emerging AI-powered tools.

Before Sephora, Emmy worked at Clorox and Yes To, experiences she credits with helping her understand brands at different stages and scales.

[02:47] When Experience is at Stake, Stores Still Win — Sephora’s physical stores remain central because the experience extends beyond access to products. Emmy points to highly trained beauty advisors, discovery-oriented store layouts, and sample rewards that let loyalty members try something new. The lesson for retailers and marketers alike is that digital convenience does not automatically replace physical retail when the store creates expertise, exploration, and value that online channels struggle to reproduce.

[06:07] Loyalty Has to Feel Personal, Not Transactional — As Beauty Insider approaches its 20th anniversary, Emmy says Sephora has kept evolving the program around choice. The birthday gift has expanded from a simple reward into multiple options, including points, because clients value choosing for themselves. That philosophy reflects the broader goal: loyalty should make customers feel recognized rather than simply rewarded for spending again.

[07:36] Personalization Can Turn Rewards Into Discovery — Beauty Insider Challenges use limited-time tasks and point rewards to encourage members to try something new. The tasks can be personalized based on purchase history. For example, prompting someone who has never bought a hair product at Sephora to explore that category. More than 30% of clients have participated in a challenge since the format launched in 2023.

[09:09] AI Shouldn’t Feel Creepy — More than 90% of Sephora’s sales come from Beauty Insider members, giving the brand a rich first-party data set. Emmy’s standard for using that data is straightforward: apply AI where it creates practical value for the client. Tools such as Beauty Skin Scan can improve shade and skincare recommendations, while the AI Beauty Chat remains optional. The technology earns its place by making the experience easier, not by showing off what it knows.

[14:31] Follow the Shopper Into the Agentic Era — Sephora does not expect every beauty journey to begin on Sephora.com. Emmy says the brand wants to be wherever people are shopping for beauty, which now includes AI-powered environments. Sephora has an app inside ChatGPT and a Google Agentic Checkout partnership that lets consumers discover and purchase without leaving those platforms. The strategy is less about protecting a destination and more about protecting relevance.

[18:12] Client-Friendly Decisions Are Not Always the Easy Ones — Emmy’s leadership philosophy comes back to a question she encountered early at Sephora: “Is this client friendly?” The answer can create additional operational complexity. Birthday gifts, rewards, and loyalty experiences require coordination across multiple departments, but Emmy argues that advocating for what clients genuinely want is part of the job, even when the simpler internal answer would be easier to execute.

Matt Britton is America’s leading expert on the millennial generation having consulted for over half of the Fortune 500 over the past two decades. He is the Founder and CEO of Suzy and the host of The Speed of Culture Podcast.

#7238. Brandweek 2026: Why Gopuff Would ‘Rather Be Debated Than Ignored’ ft. Tyler Stewart

Playing it safe is expensive when nobody notices you.

Live from the Marketing Vanguard stage at Brandweek 2026, Jenny Rooney sits down with Tyler Stewart, head of marketing at Gopuff, for a play-by-play on how a challenger brand competes in one of the most crowded corners of commerce without trying to outspend everyone else.

Tyler gets into why Gopuff’s real edge sits in its first-party logistics, and how competitors can also become distribution partners

He also addresses why the promise of “instant” means more than shaving minutes off a delivery.

This episode is part of a special vidcast series that was recorded live during Brandweek 2026 and presented in partnership with Tatari.

Tyler Stewart is head of marketing at Gopuff, where he leads marketing across the business as the company continues to expand what instant commerce can look like.

His career started in sports entertainment and talent representation before moving through media and integrated marketing roles at GQ, The Players’ Tribune and Bleacher Report.

That mix of sports, media, entertainment and startup experience now shapes how he approaches Gopuff: find where the brand can create attention, build partnerships people actually care about and turn the company’s operational strengths into something culturally relevant.

[05:32] Could Your Competitors Be Your Best Distribution Partners? — Gopuff competes with major delivery platforms, but Tyler does not view the category as adversarial. The company’s first-party logistics allow it to sell through platforms like DoorDash and Uber Eats while also serving as a last-mile partner elsewhere. Here lies a lesson for marketers – competitive advantage can create more options than simply beating everyone else at their own game.

[13:11] How Gopuff Competes Against the Giants — Tyler is blunt that Gopuff does not have the budget to match every larger competitor dollar for dollar. His answer is resourcefulness. Partnerships with talent like Tom Brady are designed to create products, access, and culturally interesting moments rather than simply rent celebrity reach.

[14:32] “We’d Rather Be Debated Than Ignored” — At Brandweek 2026, Tyler offers one of the clearest statements of Gopuff’s brand philosophy. In an attention economy where being noticed is increasingly difficult, he would rather push far enough to spark debate than play it so safely that nobody notices. For a challenger brand, invisibility is the bigger risk.

[17:14] One Deep Creator Partnership Beats Ten Logo Slaps — Tyler’s partnership strategy is “do less but better.” Instead of asking talent for another sponsored post, Gopuff tries to build something with them, whether that means products, content, or unique access. The goal is to make the relationship feel organic to both the creator and the audience.

Jenny Rooney is Chief Brand and Community Officer, leading strategy for the overall ADWEEK brand as well as the ways in which we serve and support our audiences with high-value content, products, partnerships and experiences, notably through our community programs such as Marketing Vanguard.

#7222. Velocity Over Volume: Archer Roose Wines on Building Brand Loyalty in a Competitive Market

What does it take to disrupt a legacy industry without losing sight of what consumers actually want? Sometimes, the answer lies in rethinking something as familiar as the packaging.

But turning an unconventional product idea into a lasting brand requires more than innovation. It takes a distinctive voice, genuine consumer insight, and a commitment to sustainable growth.

In this episode of Adspeak by ADWEEK, chief content officer Zoë Ruderman sits down with Marian Leitner-Waldman, founder and CEO of Archer Roose Wines, and Elizabeth Banks, actor, producer, director, and chief creative officer at Archer Roose Wines, to explore how they’re challenging conventions in the wine industry.

The conversation examines how canned wine, premium positioning, and irreverent brand storytelling are helping Archer Roose rethink wine accessibility and redefine consumer perceptions of the category.

The guests discuss how identifying an overlooked consumer frustration, the waste of unfinished wine bottles, helped inspire a different approach to wine innovation.

They also explain why brand voice matters as much as product quality, and how humor, authenticity, and a willingness to avoid condescension can build meaningful consumer engagement.

A central theme is the importance of Velocity Over Volume: prioritizing reorder metrics, customer loyalty, and sustainable growth over short-lived viral success. The discussion also explores Archer Roose’s patient partnership marketing strategy with JetBlue, demonstrating how long-term thinking can turn an unexpected opportunity into a lasting distribution channel.

Marian Leitner-Waldman is the Founder and CEO of Archer Roose Wines, where she is redefining wine accessibility through premium, single-serve canned wine. Built around the insight that consumers often want just a glass rather than an entire bottle, Archer Roose has become the No. 1 canned wine by on-premise velocity. Marian has driven its growth through consumer-led innovation, strategic partnerships, and a focus on customer loyalty. Before entering the wine industry, she worked in global health finance, raising $57 million for Gavi and helping lead a $2 billion campaign for the Global Partnership for Education.

Elizabeth Banks is an award-nominated actor, producer, director, and entrepreneur known for her versatility across film and television. Her notable credits include The Hunger Games, Pitch Perfect, Spider-Man, 30 Rock, and The 40-Year-Old Virgin. She co-founded Brownstone Productions, the company behind the Pitch Perfect franchise, and has directed films including Pitch Perfect 2 and Charlie’s Angels. Beyond entertainment, Elizabeth serves as Chief Creative Officer at Archer Roose Wines, bringing her creative perspective to the brand’s marketing, storytelling, and category innovation.

#7215. Home Depot, ESPN Among 2026 Brand Hall of Fame Inductees

The Brand Hall of Fame has inducted five new members for 2026.

The Home Depot, ESPN, Disney, Budweiser, and Chick-fil-A are the five inductees. They join the inaugural 2024 class of honorees including Coca-Cola, the NFL, Levi’s, McDonald’s, and MTV, as well as the 2025 class of Gatorade, American Express, Fender, and Dove.

The new additions, who were celebrated at the Brand Hall of Fame Awards Gala, hosted by The Gathering Festival in Banff, Canada in October, bring the total Brand Hall of Fame list to 15 companies.

Each year, five new winners are selected to the Brand Hall of Fame by a committee of senior marketers to join the Brand Hall of Fame from a list of 100 top nominees. Nominees don’t need to lead their category, but they are graded against 10 criteria to demonstrate sustained success over a long-term period.

“Every brand in this room has been tested by decades of change and is still here, still growing, still shaping how people live,” said Mandy Balak, president of The Gathering Festival and a member of the selection committee, in a statement. “That’s rare. “The Hall exists to study it as much as celebrate it, so the next generation of builders knows what it actually takes.”

Also at the Gala The One Club for Creativity honored Apple as the first brand to ever be named in its Creative Hall of Fame.

“What does it take to build a lasting creative legacy? For Apple, it’s a commitment to creativity that runs through its products, its design, its communications and the tools it puts in other people’s hands, held for decades,” said Adam Izen, chief awards officer, The One Club for Creativity. “The Creative Hall of Fame has always celebrated the people who shaped our creative world. Recognizing a brand for the first time acknowledges the role organizations play in creating the conditions for great work to happen, again and again.”

Nominations for the 2027 Brand Hall of Fame are open until Dec. 31.

#7214. Senior Deliverability and Compliance Specialist at Kit
